Alice or Bob? Process Polymorphism in Choreographies
Eva Graversen, Andrew K. Hirsch, Fabrizio Montesi

Abstract
We present PolyChor λ \lambda , a language for higher-order functional choreographic programming —an emerging paradigm by which programmers write the desired cooperative behaviour of a system of communicating processes and then compile it into distributed implementations for each process, a translation called endpoint projection . Unlike its predecessor, Chor λ \lambda , PolyChor λ \lambda has both type and process polymorphism inspired by System F ω . That is, PolyChor λ \lambda is the first (higher-order) functional choreographic language which gives programmers the ability to write generic choreographies and determine the participants at runtime. This novel combination of features also allows PolyChor λ \lambda processes to communicate distributed values , leading to a new and intuitive way to write delegation. While some of the functional features of PolyChor λ \lambda give it a weaker correspondence between the semantics of choreographies and their endpoint-projected concurrent systems than some other choreographic languages, we still get the hallmark end result of choreographic programming: projected programs are deadlock-free by design.

Introduction

Concurrent programs involve interacting processes.
Usually, programmers write one program per process, and then compose those programs in parallel.
These programs contain
send
and
receive
expressions which transmit data between processes.
This makes it easy to write code that
deadlocks
, or gets stuck because patterns of sends and receives do not match.

Session types

(
Honda 1993
;
Honda et al. 1998
)
can be used to describe the patterns of sends and receives in a program, offering a foundation for static analyses aimed at preventing communication mismatches and deadlocks
(
Scalas and Yoshida 2019
;
Caires and Pfenning 2010
;
Wadler 2012
;
DeYoung et al. 2012
;
Honda et al. 2016
;
Dardha et al. 2012
)
.
Working with session types enables the programmer to ensure the communications in their system follow compatible send/receive patterns.

Alternatively, programmers can use a
global
point of view
(
Dalla Preda et al. 2017
;
Giallorenzo et al. 2021
;
Carbone and Montesi 2013
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021
;
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Richter et al. 2022
;
Jongmans and van den Bos 2022
;
López et al. 2016
, see e.g.,)
.
A programmer using the global point of view writes one program, which is then compiled to a program for each process.

Choreographic programming

(
Montesi 2013
)
is a global programming paradigm with particularly well-explored foundations
(
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2020
;
Montesi 2023
)
.
In choreographies,
send
and
receive
commands are written together, usually using a notation inspired by security protocols
(
Needham and Schroeder 1978
)
. This has two key advantages.
First, it gives programmers the power to express the desired communication flow among processes, but without the burden of manually coding send and receive actions.
Second, it ensures that there is no mismatch which can cause deadlock, a property that has become known as
deadlock-freedom by design

(
Carbone and Montesi 2013
)
.

To see the power of this, consider the (in)famous bookseller example—a recurring example in the literature of choreographic programming and session types
(
Carbone and Montesi 2013
;
Honda et al. 2016
;
Montesi 2023
)
.

Buyer
wants to buy a book from
Seller
.
To this end,
Buyer
sends the title of the book—say, “The Importance of Being Earnest”—to
Seller
, who then sends back the price.

Buyer
then can compare the price with its budget and based on the result informs
Seller
that they want to buy the book if it is within their budget, or informs them that they do not want to buy the book otherwise.
We can describe this via the following choreography:

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0mux=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu(\text{``The Importance of Being Earnest''}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}})\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muy=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}}}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0muy<\textsf{budget}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Buy}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\right)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Quit}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\right)\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

In this choreography, as in all choreographic programs, computation takes place among multiple
processes
communicating via message passing.
Values are located at processes; for example, in the first line of the choreography, the title of the book is initially located at
Buyer
.
The function
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}
communicates a value from the process
P
to the process
Q
.
It takes a local value at
P
and returns a local value at
Q
.
1
1

1

When we formalize our language in Section
3
, we require a type annotation on
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}
, but in the introduction we leave it unannotated for the clarity of our examples.

Thus,
x
represents the string “The Importance of Being Earnest” at the process
Seller
, while
y
represents the price at the process
Buyer
.
Finally, we check locally if the book’s price is in
Buyer
’s budget.
Either way, we use function
select
to send a label from
Buyer
to
Seller
representing
Buyer
’s choice to either proceed with the purchase or not.
Either way, the choreography returns the dummy value
()
at
Seller
.

While most of the early work on choreographies focused on simple lower-order imperative programming like in the example above, recent work has shown how to develop higher-order choreographic programming languages.
These languages allow a programmer to write deadlock-free code using the usual abstractions of higher-order programming, such as objects
(
Giallorenzo et al. 2020
)
and higher-order functions
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021
)
.

For instance, the program above bakes in the title and the value of the book.
However, we may want to use this code whenever
Buyer
wants to buy any book, and let
Buyer
use any local function to decide whether to buy the book at a price.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{title}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{Bool}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0mux=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{title}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muy=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}}}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mkern 4.0muy\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Buy}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\right)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Quit}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\right)\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

A programmer using a higher-order choreographic language, like a programmer using any higher-order programming language, can write a program once and use it in a large number of situations.
For instance, by supplying different values of
title
and
buyAtPrice?
, this same code can be used to buy several different titles and
Buyer
can determine if they are willing to buy the book at the price using any method they desire.

While the move from first-order programming to higher-order programming is significant, previous work on the theoretical foundations of higher-order choreographic programming still did not account for other forms of abstraction
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021
)
.
In particular, they did not allow for
polymorphism
, where programs can abstract over types as well as data, allowing them to operate in many more settings; nor did they allow for
delegation
, where one process can ask another process to act in its stead.

These forms of abstraction are relatively standard: delegation is an important operation in concurrent calculi, and polymorphism is vital to modern programming.
In choreographic programming, however, another form of abstraction becomes natural: abstraction over processes.
Current higher-order choreographic languages require that code mention concrete process names.
However, we often want to write more-generic code, allowing the same code to run on many processes.
For example, the above program allows
Buyer
to decide whether to buy a book from
Seller
using any local function
buyAtPrice?
.
It would be more natural to write
Seller
as a book-selling
service
which different clients could interact with in the same way to buy a book.

In this paper, we tackle three styles of abstraction.
Firstly, we show that abstraction over processes is a type of polymorphism, which we refer to as
process polymorphism
.
Secondly, we extend Chor
\lambda
—a simply-typed functional choreographic language—with polymorphism, including process polymorphism, and call this new language PolyChor
\lambda
.
Thirdly, we add the ability to communicate distributed values such as functions.
Surprisingly, this addition also allows us to write code with delegation, giving a clean language to study all three forms of abstraction.

Let us examine the bookseller
service
in our extended language:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muB\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{title}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\varnothing}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{Bool}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0mux=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{B},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{title}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muy=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{B}}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mkern 4.0muy\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{B},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Buy}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\right)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{B},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Quit}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\right)\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

This program allows a process named
B
to connect with
Seller
to buy a book.

B
then provides a string
title
and a decision function
buyAtPrice?
.
Thus, we no longer have to write a separate function for every process which may want to buy a book from
Seller
.

While this addition may appear simple, it poses some unique theoretical challenges.
First, the goal of a choreographic language is to compile a global program to one local program per process.
However, since
B
does not represent any particular process, it is unclear how to compile the polymorphic code above.
We solve this problem via a simple principle: each process knows its identity.
With this principle in place, we can compile the code to a conditional in each process: one option to run if they take the role of
B
, and the other to run if they do not.

Notably, each process chooses dynamically which interpretation of the code to run.
This flexibility is important, since we may want to allow different processes to occupy
B
’s place dynamically.
For instance, we can imagine a situation where
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}
and
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}
work together to buy a particularly expensive book: perhaps they compare bank accounts, and whoever has more money buys the book for them to share.
This can be achieved in our system with the following code, where
seller_service
is the name of the function above:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{title}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0mux=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{bank\_balance}_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0mux<\textsf{bank\_balance}_{2}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Me}\mkern 4.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Me}\\
~{\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\textsf{seller\_service}&\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{title})\\
&({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0muz\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muz<\textsf{bank\_balance}_{2})\end{array}\right)}\end{array}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{You}\mkern 4.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Them}\\
~{(\textsf{seller\_service}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Buyer}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{title}\mkern 4.0mu({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0muz\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muz<x))}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

A related challenge shows up in the operational semantics of our extended language.
Languages like PolyChor
\lambda
generally have operational semantics which match the semantics of the compiled code by allowing
out-of-order execution
: redices in different processes might be reduced in any order.
However, care must be taken with process polymorphism, since it may not be clear whether two redices are in the same or different processes.

In addition to type and process polymorphism, PolyChor
\lambda
is the first choreographic language to allow the communication of distributed values: values not located entirely at the sender. These values include full choreographies described by distributed functions, which can be used to model delegation.
To see how process polymorphism and communication of distributed values enables delegation, consider the following scenario:
when a buyer asks for a book, the seller first checks whether it is in stock.
If it is, the sale continues as normal.
If not, the seller delegates to a second seller, which may sell the book to the buyer.
We can define this code as follows:

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muB\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{title}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{\varnothing}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{Bool}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0mux=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{B},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{title}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{found}{(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux}\mkern 4.0mu)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},{B}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Continue}\\
~{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Disconnect}\\
~{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muy=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{B}}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{price}{(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux)}\mkern 4.0mu)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mkern 4.0muy\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{B},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Buy}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}B\right)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{B},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Quit}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}B\right)\end{array}\end{array}}\end{array}}\end{array}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},{B}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Delegate}\\
~{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Delegate}\\
~{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muF=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\left(\begin{array}[t]{l}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{title}_{2}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{found}{(\textsf{price\_lookup}_{2}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{title}_{2}}\mkern 4.0mu)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},{B}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Continue}\\
~{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muy^{\prime}=\textsf{price}{(\textsf{price\_lookup}_{2}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{title}_{2})}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muy=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{B}}\mkern 4.0muy^{\prime}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mkern 4.0muy\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{B},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Buy}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}B\right)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{B},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Quit}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}B\right)\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}}\end{array}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},{B}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Quit}\mkern 4.0mu\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}B\right)\end{array}\end{array}\right)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{title}_{2}=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}\mkern 4.0mux\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~F\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{title}_{2}\end{array}\end{array}}\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Structure of the Paper.

We begin in Section
2
by examining the system model of PolyChor
\lambda
.
We then proceed with the following contributions:

•

In Section
3
, we describe the PolyChor
\lambda
language in detail.
This language includes both type polymorphism and process polymorphism.
We develop both a type system and kind system and an operational semantics for PolyChor
\lambda
.

•

In Section
4
, we describe the local
network language
used to describe the distributed implementation.
We also detail how to obtain this implementation via
endpoint projection
, which compiles PolyChor
\lambda
programs to a program for each process.

•

In Section
5
we describe the main theorem of this paper, the correctness of endpoint projection with respect to our operational semantics.
Because of the dynamic nature of process polymorphism, this requires significant reasoning compared to previous works on choreographies.

Finally, we discuss related work in Section
6
and conclude in Section
7
.

2
System Model

We begin by discussing the assumptions we make about how PolyChor
\lambda
programs will be run.
These assumptions are as light as possible, allowing for PolyChor
\lambda
to be run in many different scenarios.
In particular, we assume that we have a fixed set of processes, which can communicate via messages.
These processes can each be described by a polymorphic
\lambda
-calculus, similar to System F
\omega
, but with the addition of communication primitives.

2.1
Processes

We assume that there is a fixed set
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathbf{N}}
of process names
P
,
Q
,
Alice
, et cetera.
These processes can represent nodes in a distributed system, system processes, threads, or more.
Process polymorphism allows us to refer to processes using type variables, which may go in or out of scope.
Despite this, the set of physically-running processes remains the same.

We assume every process knows its identity.
Thus, every process can choose what code to run on the basis of its identity.
This assumption is reasonable for many practical settings, for instance it is common for nodes in distributed systems to know their identity.
This capability is essential to our strategy for enabling process polymorphism.

2.2
Communication

We assume that process communicate via synchronous message passing.
Thus, if
P
sends a message to
Q
, then
P
does not continue until
Q
has received the message.
Moreover, we assume that message passing is instantaneous and certain, so messages do not get lost.

Processes can receive two kinds of messages: values of local programs (described below) and
labels
describing choices made during a computation.
These are used to ensure that different processes stay in lock-step with each other.

2.3
Local Programs

We assume that processes run a
local
language described in Section
4
.
This is a functional language extended with communication features, similar to the language GV
(
Gay and Vasconcelos 2010
;
Wadler 2012
)
.
However, unlike GV or its derivatives, our local language includes type and process polymorphism.

Endpoint projection translates PolyChor
\lambda
into this “Network Process” language.
We have thus further extended GV with features required for our endpoint-projection mechanism.
For instance, we provide an
AmI
expression form, which allows a process to choose which code to run based on its identity.
Despite these extensions, the language should feel familiar to any reader familiar with polymorphic
\lambda
-calculi.

3
The Polymorphic Chor
\lambda
Language

We now turn to our first major contribution: the design of the polymorphic, choreographic
\lambda
-calculus, PolyChor
\lambda
.
This calculus extends the choreographic
\lambda
-calculus Chor
\lambda
of
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021
with both type and, more importantly, process polymorphism.
We begin by describing the features that PolyChor
\lambda
shares with the base Chor
\lambda
before describing the new features.
The syntax of PolyChor
\lambda
can be found in Figure
1
.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\textrm{Variables}&x,y,\ldots\\
\textrm{Type Variables}&X,Y,\ldots\\
\textrm{Integers}&n\\
\textrm{Labels}&\ell\\
\textrm{Process Names}&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\\
\textrm{Process-Name Sets}&{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}&\in&2^{\text{Type Values}}nds]{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}}&&\,\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}pes]{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\\
&&\,\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}peValues]{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\,\mid\,X\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\\
&&\,\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}pressions]{M,N,\ldots}\,\mid\,x\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,n\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM\\
&&\,\mid&M\mkern 4.0muN\,\mid\,M\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muM\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muM\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}case}}\mkern 5.0muM\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}of}}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{~}\mkern 4.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}N_{1};~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{~}\mkern 4.0muy\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}N_{2}\\
&&\,\mid&{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}M{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muN{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}fst}}\mkern 4.0muM\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}snd}}\mkern 4.0muM\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}},\mkern 2.0mu{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\ell\mkern 4.0muM\,\mid\,flues]{V}\,\mid\,x\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,n\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muV\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muV\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}V_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muV_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}},\mkern 2.0mu{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}}\end{array}

Figure 1:
PolyChor
\lambda
Syntax

Syntax Inherited from Chor
\lambda
.

Since choreographic programs describe the behavior of an entire communicating network of processes, we need to reason about where terms are located.
In other words, we need to know which processes store the data denoted by a term.
Terms of base type, like integers, are stored by exactly one process.
This is represented in our type system by matching base types with a process name.
For example, integers stored by the process
Alice
are represented by the type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}
.
Values of this type also mark the process which stores them, so a value
5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}
( read “the integer
5
at
Alice
”) has type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}
.
In Figure
1
, the only base types are
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
, but it is easy to extend the language with other base types, such as the types
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{Bool}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
used in the introduction.
We will continue to freely use other base types in our examples.

While base types are located on just one process, data of more-complex types may involve multiple processes.
For instance, the term
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0mu42\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
involves both data stored by
Alice
and
Bob
.
This is still recorded in the type: the term above has type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}
.
In addition to base types and product types, PolyChor
\lambda
also has sum types (written
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
), along with their normal introduction and elimination forms.

Functions are treated more unusually: while we have standard
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}
and application forms, we also allow functions to be defined mutually-recursively with each other.
In order to do so, any PolyChor
\lambda
choreography is associated with a list,
D
, of bindings of functions to
function variables

f
, which are also expressions. A function variable can then during execution be instantiated with its definition according to this list.
As we will see in Section
3.3
, PolyChor
\lambda
terms are evaluated in a context which associates each function variable with a term.
Note that, while in the original Chor
\lambda
types were mutually recursive in a similar way, in PolyChor
\lambda
we do not support recursive types.
To see why, note that we syntactically restrict many types to
type values
.
This prevents us having to reason about processes denoted by arbitrary terms—e.g., we cannot send to the “process”
({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0muX)\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
but we can write
({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muY\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}},\mkern 2.0mu{Y}})\mkern 4.0mu(({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0muX)\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})
which, due to our call-by-value semantics, will force the type to reduce to
P
before
Y
gets instantiated.
As we will see in Section
4
, allowing communication between arbitrary types would make endpoint projection difficult.
However, since recursive types cannot necessarily reduce to a type value, they cannot be used in many parts of the type system.

Function types are also more specific than their usual construction in
\lambda
-calculus: they are written
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
.
Here,
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
is the set of processes which describes which processes, other than those in
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
, may participate in the function body (note that
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
may contain type variables).
Thus, if
Alice
wants to communicate an integer to
Bob
directly (without intermediaries), then she should use a function of type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\varnothing}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}
.
However, if she is willing to use the process
Proxy
as an intermediary, then she should use a function of type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Proxy}}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}
.

In order to allow values to be communicated between processes, we provide the primitive communication function
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}
.
This function takes a value of type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
at
P
and returns the corresponding type at
Q
.
As mentioned in the introduction, most choreographic languages provide a communication term modeled after the “Alice-and-Bob” notation of cryptographic protocols.
For instance,
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}\mathbin{\texttt{->}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}\colon 5
might represent
Alice
sending
5
to
Bob
.
This is easily recovered by applying the function
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}}}
.
For example, the term
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}}}\mkern 4.0mu(5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}})
represents
Alice
sending a message containing
5
to
Bob
:
it evaluates to
5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}
and has type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}
.

Finally, consider the following, where
M
has type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}
:

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}case}}\mkern 5.0muM\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}of}}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{~}\mkern 4.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}3\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}};\\
\mkern 10.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{~}\mkern 4.0muy\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}4\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}\end{array}

Clearly,
Bob
needs to know which branch is taken, since he needs to store a different return value in each branch.
However, only
Alice
knows which whether
M
evaluates to
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}}\mkern 4.0muV
or
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}}\mkern 4.0muV
(here
inl
and
inr
are used to denote that a value is either the right or left part of a sum and annotated with the type of the other part of the sum to ensure type principality).
Thus, this choreography cannot correspond to any network program. Using the terminology found in the literature of choreographic languages, we might say that the choreography is
unrealisable
because there is insufficient
knowledge of choice

(
Castagna et al. 2012
;
Montesi 2023
)
.

In order to enable programs where a process’s behaviour differs depending on other processes data, such as how
Bob
behaved differently depending on
Alice
’s data, we provide
select
terms.
These allow one process to tell another which branch has been taken, preventing knowledge from “appearing out of nowhere.”
For instance, we can extend the program above to:

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}case}}\mkern 5.0muM\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}of}}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{~}\mkern 4.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Left}\mkern 4.0mu(3\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}});\\
\mkern 10.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{~}\mkern 4.0muy\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Right}\mkern 4.0mu(4\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}})\end{array}

This represents the same program as above, except
Alice
tells
Bob
whether the left or the right branch has been taken.
Unlike the previous version of this example, it
does
represent a (deadlock-free) network program.
In general, we allow arbitrary labels to be sent by
select
terms, so semantically-meaningful labels can be chosen.

While
com
and
select
both transfer information between two processes, they differ in what information they transfer.
com
moves a value, e.g., as an integer or a function, from the sender to the receiver.
select
on the other hand uses a label to inform the receiver of a choice made by the sender. Some choreographic languages combine the two, so both a label and a value is communicated at the same time, but like most choreographic languages PolyChor
\lambda
keeps the two separate.

Syntax Additions over Chor
\lambda
.

In order to achieve (both type and process) polymorphism in PolyChor
\lambda
, we add several features based on System F
\omega

(
Girard 1972
)
.
In particular, we add kinds and universal types
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
along with type abstraction and application.
From System F
\omega
we inherit the kind
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}
, which is the kind of types.
We additionally inherit the kind
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}
which represents functions from types to types.

Moreover, we inherit type-level functions
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
from System F
\omega
.
These represent the definition of type constructors.
We additionally have type-level function application
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
.
Since types contain computation, we also define type
values
, which are simply types without application.

Note that the base types
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
, like local values, are
syntactically
restricted to only allow type values as subterms.
This allows us to use a type variable to compute the location of a value dynamically, but not arbitrary terms, which would make it much harder to tell at time of projection where the value is located.
Thus, we can write
({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X)\mkern 4.0mu(Y\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})
to compute the location of an integer dynamically (
Y\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
has to reduce to a type value before
X
can be instantiated), but we cannot write
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}(Y\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})
directly.
This way, our projected calculus can tell when instantiating
X
(at runtime) whether it gets instantiated as
P
.
It would be more complicated to create runtime checks for whether
Y
gets instantiated as a function type that outputs
P
or not.

In addition to the kinds
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}
of System F
\omega
, we also have the kind
Proc
of
process names
.
Thus, process names are types, but they cannot be used to type any terms.
Additionally, we have kinds
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
, which represents types of kind
K
which do not mention any of the processes in the set
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
.
Since we restrict the types that can be communicated based on which processes they contain, as we will see soon, this restricted kind can be used to define polymorphic functions which contain communication.
For instance, the term

{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muY\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}\{X\}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X}_{{X},\mkern 2.0mu{Y}}\mkern 4.0mu(5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}X)

defines a function which, given
distinct
processes
X
and
Y
, causes
X
to send
5
to
Y
.

In the rest of this section, we explore the semantics of PolyChor
\lambda
.
First, we look at its static semantics, both in the form of typing and kinding.
Second, we describe its operational semantics.
Throughout, we will continue to give intuitions based on the concurrent interpretation of PolyChor
\lambda
, though the semantics we give here does not correspond directly to that interpretation.

3.1
Typing

We now turn to the type system for PolyChor
\lambda
.
As before, our type system builds on that for Chor
\lambda
.
Here, we focus on the rules that are new in this work.
Thus, we focus on rules related to polymorphism, and those that have had to change due to polymorphism.

Typing judgements for PolyChor
\lambda
have the form
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
, where
\Theta
is the set of process names—either names in
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathbf{N}}
or type variables with kind
Proc
—used in
M
or the type of
M
.
The
typing environment

\Gamma
is a list associating variables and function names to their types and type variables and process names to their kinds.
We sometimes refer to the pair
\Theta;\Gamma
as a
typing context
.

\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\quad\textsc{left=Tunit}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash n\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\quad\textsc{left=Tint}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash N:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash N~M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\quad\textsc{left=Tapp}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\quad\Theta;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\text{ for all }{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\in\rho\quad\Theta\cap({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}})\cup\text{ftv}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}})\cup\text{ftv}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}));\Gamma,x:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\quad}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\quad\textsc{left=Tabs}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\ell\mkern 4.0muM:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\quad\textsc{left=Tsel}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})\cup\text{ftv}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}))\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})\cup\text{ftv}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}))}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}},\mkern 2.0mu{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}}:({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\varnothing}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}})}\quad\textsc{left=Tcom}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}[X\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}]}\quad\textsc{left=TappT}\frac{\Theta^{\prime};\Gamma^{\prime},X::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\quad\text{if}\;\exists{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\mathrel{\text{then}}\Gamma^{\prime}=(\Gamma+X)\mathop{\&}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\setminus X\mathrel{\text{else}}\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma+X\quad\text{if}\;{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{\text{or}}\exists{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\mathrel{\text{then}}\Theta^{\prime}=\Theta\cup\{X\}\mathrel{\text{else}}\Theta^{\prime}=\Theta}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\quad\textsc{left=TabsT}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\quad\textsc{left=Teq}

Figure 2:
Typing Rules (Selected)

Selected rules for our type system can be found in Figure
2
.
The full collection of rules are given in Appendix
A
. Again, many of the rules are inherited directly from Chor
\lambda

(
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021
)
; we thus focus on the rules that have changed due to our additions.
Many, if not most, of these rules are inspired by System F
\omega
.
However, the addition of the kind of processes and Without kinds—i.e., kinds of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
—also lead to some changes.

The rules
[
Tunit
]
and
[
Tint
]
give types to values of base types.
Here, we have to ensure that the location of the term is a process.
Intuitively, then, we want the location to have kind
Proc
.
However, it might have restrictions—that is, it might be of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
.
In this case, our subkinding system (which you can find details about in Section
3.2
) still allows us to apply the rule.

We express function application and abstraction via the
[
Tapp
]
and
[
Tabs
]
rules, respectively.
The application rule
[
Tapp
]
is largely standard—the only addition is the addition of a set
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
on the function type, as discussed earlier.
The abstraction rule
[
Tabs
]
, on the other hand, is more complicated.
First, it ensures that the argument type,
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
, has kind
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}
.
Then, it ensures that every element in the set decorating the arrow is a process name—i.e., that it has kind
Proc
.
Finally, it checks that, in an extended environment, the body of the function has the output type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
.
As is usual, this extended environment gives a type to the argument.
However, it restricts the available process names to those in the set
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
and those mentioned in the types
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
.

There are two ways that a type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
can mention a process: it can either name it directly, or it can name it via a type variable.
Thus, in the rule
[
Tabs
]
we allow the free variables of
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
to remain in the process context, computing them using the (standard) free-type-variable function where
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0muM
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0muM
both bind
X
.
However, we must also identify the
involved processes
in a type, which we write
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})
and compute as follows:

{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}(X)={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\varnothing}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}){\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})\cup({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathbf{N}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}){\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathbf{N}}~\text{if}~\nexists{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}

The involved processes of other types are defined homomorphically.

The communication primitives
select
and
com
are typed with
[
Tsel
]
and
[
Tcom
]
, respectively.
A term
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\ell\mkern 4.0muM
behaves as
M
, where the process
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
informs the process
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
that the
\ell
branch has been taken, as we saw earlier.
Thus, the entire term has type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
if
M
does.
Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
must be processes.

The rule
[
Tcom
]
types
com
terms. So far we have been simplifying the type used in
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}
for readability. We have been using
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
to denote the input type, but as it turns out to type
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}
correctly, we have to complicate things a little.
Intuitively, a term
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}},\mkern 2.0mu{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}}\mkern 4.0muM
represents
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
communicating the parts of
M
on
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
.
Thus, we require that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
be a type
transformer
requiring a process.
Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
cannot be mentioned in
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
; otherwise not every part of the type of
M
on
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
in our example above would transfer to
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
.
For this we use the following notion of
mentioned processes
:

{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}(X)={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\varnothing}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}){\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})={\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{mp}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})~\text{if}~\nexists{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}

Again, with other types being defined homomorphically.
The difference between involved and mentioned processes is subtle. If there is no polymorphism, they are the same, but when dealing with polymorphism with restriction they are opposites: involved processes includes every process not in the restriction (the variable could be instantiated as something involving those processes and thus they may be involved), while mentioned names includes the processes mentioned in the restriction. Mentioned names is used only when typing
com
.
If we have such a type-level function,
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
, and two type values
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
which are not and will not be instantiated to anything mentioned in
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
then we can type
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}},\mkern 2.0mu{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}}\mkern 4.0mu
as a function from
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
.
Since this is direct communication, no intermediaries are necessary and we can associate this arrow with the empty set
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\varnothing}
.

It is worth noting at this point that the communication rule inspired our use of System F
\omega
rather than plain System F, which lacks type-level computation.
In Chor
\lambda
and other previous choreographic languages, communicated values must be local to the sender.
In PolyChor
\lambda
, this would mean not allowing the communicated type to include type variables or processes other than the sender.
Since we are introducing the idea of using communication as a means of delegation, we have slackened that restriction.
This means that PolyChor
\lambda
programs can communicate larger choreographies whose type may involve other processes, and importantly other type variables.
We see this in the delegation example at the end of Section
1
, where we have the communication
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}
.
Adding in the required type annotation (which we had suppressed in the introduction), this becomes
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{\varnothing}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}
.
Note that this still leaves us with a free type variable
B
, representing the unknown process that
Seller
is telling
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}
to interact with!
Since we cannot ban free type variables in communicated types, we must create a typing system that can handle them, and this requires type level computation.

To see why this led us to type-level computation, consider the alternative.
In Chor
\lambda
and other choreographic works, we would have a type communication using process
substitution
instead of communication.
The annotated program would then be
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{\varnothing}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}
.
When applied to a program of appropriate type, the result would have type

({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{\varnothing}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B)[\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}]={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{\varnothing}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B

Note that, because
B
is a type variable, it was ignored by the substitution.
If
B
is later substituted with something which contains
Seller
, then we have lost the information that we need to change this to
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}
.
Thus, we need some mechanism to delay this substitution; rather than use a mechanism like explicit substitutions, we instead reached for the standard tool of System F
\omega
.
This seemed more elegant and less ad-hoc; moreover, it adds features which a real-world implementation of PolyChor
\lambda
would want anyway.

Returning now to the typing rules of Figure
2
, we next have the
[
TappT
]
and
[
TabsT
]
rules, which type universal quantification.
The
[
TappT
]
rule is completely standard, while the
[
TabsT
]
rule is our most-complicated rule. In this rule, we have 4 different definitions for the typing context of
M
, depending on the kind of
X
.
As is standard, we check if the body of the function has the right type when the parameter
X
has kind
K
.
But first, if
X
is a process, then we need to extend
\Theta
with
X
.
In addition, we must further manipulate the context in order to ensure that the types whose kinds are restricted of
X
correspond to the restriction on the kind of
X
.

First, the new type variable
X
may shadow a previously-defined
X
.
Thus, we need to remove
X
from any restrictions already in the context.
We do this using the following operation
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}+{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
:

({\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho})+{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}=({\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}+{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\{}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\}})

We define
+
on other kinds homomorphically, and extend this to contexts as usual:

\Gamma+{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}=\{x:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mid x:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\in\Gamma\}\cup\{X:{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}+{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\mid X:{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\in\Gamma\}

Furthermore, if
X
itself has a Without kind—that is,
X
’s kind tells us it cannot be any of the processes in
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
—then we need to symmetrically add a restriction on
X
to every type in
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
.
Otherwise, we would not be able to use the roles in
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
in any place where we cannot use
X
, even though we know
X
will not be instantiated with them.
We do this with the operation
\Gamma\mathop{\&}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\setminus X
, which we define as follows:

\Gamma\mathop{\&}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\setminus X=\begin{array}[t]{l}\{x:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mid x:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\in\Gamma\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\in\Gamma\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\}\\
{}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{2}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\{}{X}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{2}}\in\Gamma\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\}\\
{}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\{}{X}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\in\Gamma\text{, }{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{2}}\text{, and }{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\}\end{array}

With these operations in place, we can now fully understand
[
TabsT
]
.
When
K
is actually a Without kind, then we must handle both shadowing and symmetrical restrictions.
However, when it is not a Without kind, we must only handle shadowing.

The final addition to our type system is the rule
[
Teq
]
.
This is another standard rule from System F
\omega
; it tells us that we are allowed to compute in types.
More specifically, it tells us that we can replace a type with an equivalent type, using the following equivalence:
