\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}}\\
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}{{}^{\prime}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}{{}^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}{{}^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}{{}^{\prime}}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}{{}^{\prime}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}{{}^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}{{}^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}{{}^{\prime}}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}{{}^{\prime}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}{{}^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}{{}^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}{{}^{\prime}}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau^{\prime}}}\frac{}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}})\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}[X\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}]}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}{{}^{\prime}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}{{}^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}{{}^{\prime}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}{{}^{\prime}}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau^{\prime}}}

While
[
Teq
]
is the last rule we added to our type system, it is not the last rule in Figure
2
.
We also add an extra judgement of the form
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash D
where
\Theta;\Gamma
is a typing context as before, and
D
is a set of definitions for function variables—i.e.,
D=\{f_{1}=M_{1},\ldots f_{n}=M_{n}\}
.
We write
D(f)
for the term associated with
f
in
D
.
The only rule for this judgement is
[
Tdefs
]
, which says that a set of definitions is well-formed if every variable in
D
is associated with a type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
in
\Gamma
, and the body of
f
in
D
can be given be given type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
in the context
\emptyset;\Gamma
.
We require that the body of
f
can be typed with an empty set of roles because they are global predefined functions, and as such they should not be local to any one process.

\frac{\forall f\in\mathsf{domain}(D).\,f\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\in\Gamma\land\emptyset;\Gamma\vdash D(f)\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash D}\quad\textsc{left=Tdefs}

3.2
Kinding

We finish our discussion of the static semantics of PolyChor
\lambda
by looking at our kinding system.
Our kinding system uses only one judgement,
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
, which says that in the typing context
\Theta;\Gamma
, the type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
has kind
K
.
You can find the rules of our kinding system in Figure
3
.
These are mostly directly inherited from System F
\omega
.
However, we must account for
Proc
and Without kinds.

\frac{X::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\in\Gamma\quad\text{if}\;{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}},{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\}\mathrel{\text{then}}X\in\Theta}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash X::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}}\quad\textsc{left=Kvar}\frac{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\in\Gamma\quad{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}},{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\}\quad\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\in\Theta\quad\text{if}\;{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\mathrel{\text{then}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}}\quad\textsc{left=Krole}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}\quad\textsc{left=Kunit}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}\quad\textsc{left=Kint}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{2}}\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{2}}\quad\forall{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{1}}.\,\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{2}}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{2}}}\quad\textsc{left=Kfun}\frac{\Theta^{\prime};\Gamma,X::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}}\quad\textsc{left=Kabs}\frac{\Theta^{\prime};\Gamma,X::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}\quad\textsc{left=Kall}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::({\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}({\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho})}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::({\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}\quad\textsc{left=Karr}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}}\quad\textsc{left=Ksub}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}\quad\textsc{left=Ksum}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\quad\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}{\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}\quad\textsc{left=Kprod}

Figure 3:
Kinding Rules

For instance, the rules
[
Kunit
]
and
[
Kint
]
check that the type representing which process is storing the data indeed has the kind
Proc
.
Similarly,
[
Kfun
]
ensures that all of the types in the set of possible intermediaries are processes.
The rule for type variables,
[
Kvar
]
, ensures that if a type variable
X
is assigned kind
Proc
, then
X
must also be in
\Theta
.

One of the biggest differences between our kinding system and that of System F
\omega
, however, is the rule
[
Ksub
]
which tells us that our system enjoys
subkinding
.
The subkinding rules come from the subset ordering on Without kinds.
The rules for subkinding are as follows:

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}}\quad\textsc{left=SKRefl}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{3}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{3}}}}\quad\textsc{left=SKTrans}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}^{\prime}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}^{\prime}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}\quad}{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}^{\prime}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}^{\prime}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}}\quad\textsc{left=SKArr}\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\varnothing}}\quad\textsc{left=SKEmpty}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}}\quad\textsc{left=SKWithoutL}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{1}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{2}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}<:}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho_{1}}}\quad\textsc{left=SKWithoutUnion}

Lemma 1
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
be a type.
If there exists a typing context
\Theta;\Gamma
such that
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
then there exists a unique type value
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
.

Proof.

The existence of
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
follows from induction on
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
and its uniqueness from induction on
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
.
∎

Lemma 2

(Type restriction)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
be a type.
If there exists a typing context
\Theta;\Gamma
such that
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
then
({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})\cup\text{ftv}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}))\cap{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}=\emptyset

Proof.

Follows from kinding rules.
∎

Theorem 1

(Kindable types)
.

Let
M
be a choreography and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
be a type such that
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
.
Then
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\ast}
.

Proof.

Follows from induction on the derivation of
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
and the kinding rules.
∎

We also find that types have the same kinds as their equivalent type values.
Due to
\beta
-expansion, a kindable type can be equivalent to an unkindable type, but not an unkindable type value.

Theorem 2

(Kind Preservation)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
be a type.
If there exists a typing context
\Theta;\Gamma
such that
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
, then
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
for any type value
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
.

Proof.

Follows from the kinding and type equivalence rules.
We take advantage of the fact that if we use the rule
({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}})\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}[X\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}]
to create an unkindable
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
with an extra application, then we must also use the same rule to remove this new type application before we get to a base type.
∎

Example 1
.

We return to the delegation example from Section
1
and try to type it.
As
B
appears free in the type of a value,
F
, being communicated between
Seller
and
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}
,
B
must actually have the restricted kind
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}\{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}},\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}\}
.
The choreography therefore gets the type

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}B\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}\{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}},\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}\}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\\
\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{String}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{\{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}},\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}\}}}(({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\varnothing}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{Bool}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{\{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}},\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Seller}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}\}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}B)\end{array}

This type shows both the input, output, and involved roles of the choreography.

3.3
Operational Semantics

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}{({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM)\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\to_{D}{M}[X\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}]}\quad\textsc{left=AppTAbs}\frac{M_{1}\to_{D}M_{2}}{M_{1}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\to_{D}M_{2}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\quad\textsc{left=MTApp1}\\
\frac{}{f\to_{D}D(f)}\quad\textsc{left=Def}\\
\frac{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\ell\mkern 4.0muM\to_{D}M}\quad\textsc{left=Sel}\frac{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}\mkern 4.0muV\to_{D}{V}[\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}]}\quad\textsc{left=Com}

Figure 4:
Semantics of PolyChor
\lambda
(Selected Rules)

Finally, we consider the operational semantics of PolyChor
\lambda
.
These are mostly a standard call-by-value reduction semantics for a typed
\lambda
calculus.
However, the reduction semantics must also carry a set
D
of function definitions.
Only a few rules are unusual or must be modified; those can be found in Figure
4
.
You can find the rest of the rules in Appendix
B
.

The rules
[
AppTAbs
]
and
[
MTApp1
]
come from System F
\omega
.
The rule
[
AppTAbs
]
is similar to ordinary CBV
\beta
reduction, but tells us how to reduce a
type
abstraction applied to a
type
value, but with the caveat that if we do not have a type value we must use type equivalence to get one before reducing.
The rule
[
MTApp1
]
tells us that we can reduce a type function applied to any argument.

The rule
[
Def
]
allows us to reduce function names by looking up their definition in the set
D
.

Finally, we have the rules for communication.
The rule
[
Sel
]
says that
select
acts as a no-op, as we stated earlier.
While this may seem redundant, such terms are vital for projection, as we will see in the next section.
More importantly, the
[
Com
]
rule says tells us how we represent communication at the choreography level: via substitution of roles.
This also helps explain some of the restrictions in
[
Tcom
]
.
Since we replace
all
mentions of
P
with
Q
in
V
, we cannot allow other mentions of
P
in the type transformer of
V
.
Otherwise, there could be some mentions of
P
which should not be replaced during communication, which we do not model. Unlike when typing
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}\mkern 4.0muV
, when executing a communication we know (since we only consider choreographies without free variables) that any type variables in
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
or
V
have already been instantiated and as such do we do not need to consider how to substitute variables which may later be instantiated to
P
or
Q
.

It may be surprising to learn that our semantics are simply call-by-value reduction semantics, especially for those readers familiar with choreographies.
After all, choreographies are supposed to represent concurrent programs, and so multiple redices should be available at any time.
Indeed, previous works on choreographic programming
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2020
;
Carbone and Montesi 2013
, e.g.)
provided a semantics with
out-of-order execution
, so that the operational semantics of the choreographies matched with all possible reductions in the concurrent interpretation.
We use these simpler semantics, without out-of-order execution, instead.
In exchange, our result in Section
5
will be weaker: we only promise that any value which the choreography can reduce to, so can the concurrent interpretation.

To see why we chose to obtain this weaker result, consider the choreography

f\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}}}\mkern 4.0mu(3\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}})){\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0mu(4\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}){\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}

Here we have a function
f
which needs to be instantiated with a distributed pair.
P
is ready to feed its part of the argument into
f
and start computing the result, while
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}
and
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}2}}}
are still working on computing their part of the argument.
There are two ways we could interpret PolyChor
\lambda
concurrently: we can synchronize when all processes enter a function
or
we can allow
P
to enter the function early.
We take the second, more practical, route.
However, this means it is not possible to reflect at least one evaluation order into the semantics of the choreography without banning distributed values or allowing us to somehow call a single value in multiple steps.
This insight led to us adopting the weaker guarantee discussed above.

As is standard for call-by-value
\lambda
-calculi, we are able to show that our type system is
sound
with respect to our operational semantics, as expressed in the following two theorems:

Theorem 3

(Type Preservation)
.

Let
M
be a choreography and
D
a function mapping containing every function in
M
.
If there exists a typing context
\Theta;\Gamma
such that
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
and
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash D
, then
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M^{\prime}:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
for any
M^{\prime}
such that
M\rightarrow_{D}M^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Follows from the typing and semantic rules and Theorem
2
.
∎

Theorem 4

(Progress)
.

Let
M
be a closed choreography and
D
a function mapping containing every function in
M
.
If there exists a typing context
\Theta;\Gamma
such that
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
and
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash D
, then either
M=V
or there exists
M^{\prime}
such that
M\rightarrow_{D}M^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Follows from the typing and semantic rules.
∎

4
Endpoint Projection

We now proceed to the most-important result for any choreographic programming language:
endpoint projection
.
Endpoint projection gives a concurrent interpretation to our language PolyChor
\lambda
by translating it to a parallel composition of programs, one for each process.
In order to define endpoint projection, though, we must define our process language, which we refer to as a
local
language.
The syntax of the local language can be found in Figure
5
.
There you can also find the syntax of local transition labels and network transition labels, both of which will be described when we describe the operational semantics of networks.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\textrm{Variables}&x,y,\ldots\\
\textrm{Type Variables}&X,Y,\ldots\\
\textrm{Process Names}&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}calTransitionLabels]{\mu}&&\,\mid&\tau\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mkern 4.0muV~V^{\prime}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mkern 4.0muV^{\prime}~V\\
&&\,\mid&\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\oplus}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}~\ell~}\,\mid\,\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}~\ell tworkTransitionLabels]{\mu}\,\mid\,\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}ocesslabels]{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}},\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}pes]{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{AmI}}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}?}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\\
&&\,\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}peValues]{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\,\mid\,X\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}\\
&&\,\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}pressions]{M,N,\dots}\,\mid\,x\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\,\mid\,n\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM\\
&&\,\mid&M_{1}\mkern 4.0muM_{2}\,\mid\,M\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muM\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muM\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}case}}\mkern 5.0muM\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}of}}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{~}\mkern 4.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}M_{1};~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{~}\mkern 4.0muy\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}M_{2}\\
&&\,\mid&{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}M_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muM_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}fst}}\mkern 4.0muM\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}snd}}\mkern 4.0muM\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}}~\{\ell_{1}:M_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:M_{n}\}\,\mid\,\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\oplus}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}~\ell~M}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathsf{sub}[{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}]\,\mid\,f\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}M_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}M_{2}lues]{V}\,\mid\,x\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}\,\mid\,n\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muV\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muV\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}V_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muV_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\,\mid\,\mathsf{sub}[{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}]\end{array}

Figure 5:
Local Language Syntax

As in PolyChor
\lambda
, our local language inherits much of its structure from System F
\omega
.
In particular, we have products, sums, functions, universal quantification, and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}
types, along with their corresponding terms.
In fact, some types look more like standard System F
\omega
than PolyChor
\lambda
:
function types do not need a set of processes which may participate in the function, and base types no longer need a location.

However, not everything is familiar; we have introduced new terms and new types.
The terms
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}
and
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}
allow terms to send and receive values, respectively.
We also split
select
terms into two terms: an
offer
term
\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}}~\{\ell_{1}:L_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:L_{n}\}
which allows
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
to choose how this term will evolve.
We represent such choices using
choice
terms of the form
\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\oplus}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}~\ell~L}
.
This term informs the process represented by
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
that it should reduce to its subterm labeled by
\ell
, and then itself reduces to the term
L
.
While these are unusual pieces of a polymorphic language like System F
\omega
, they are familiar from process languages like
\pi
calculus.
We also add undefined types and terms, written
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
, respectively.
These represent terms which are ill-defined; we use them to represent data which does not exist on some process
P
, but which needs to be written structurally in
P
’s program.

We also include a more-unusual feature:
explicit substitutions
of processes.
The term
\mathsf{sub}[{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}]
is a function which, when applied, replaces the role denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
with that denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
in its argument.
This function allows us to represent the view of communication according to third parties: the roles simply change, without any mechanism necessary.
For instance, imagine that
Alice
wants to tell
Bob
to communicate an integer to
Cathy
.
She can do this by sending
Bob
the function
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Cathy}}}}
.
In PolyChor
\lambda
, this corresponds to the choreography

\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\to}_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\varnothing}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Cathy}}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\left(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Cathy}}}}\right)

In order to project this choreography, we need to be able to project the communication function above even when it is not applied to any arguments.
This is where we use explicit substitutions: we project the communication function to
\mathsf{sub}[\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}]
.

Finally, we introduce our unique feature:
AmI
terms and their corresponding type.
These represent the ability of a process to know its own identity, and to take actions based on that knowledge. Process polymorphism requires an instantiation of a process variable at process
P
to be accompanied by a conditional determining whether the variable has been instantiated as
P
or as some other process
P
may interact with.
In particular, the term
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}M_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}M_{2}
reduces to
M_{1}
if the term is run by the process denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
, and
M_{2}
otherwise.
Since
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
may have different types, we provide types of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{AmI}}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}?}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
, which represent either the type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
(if typing a term on the process denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
) or
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
(otherwise).
These terms form a backbone of endpoint projection for PolyChor
\lambda
: every
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}
term binding a process gets translated to include an
AmI
term.
For instance, consider projecting the choreography

{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X^{\prime}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}},\mkern 2.0mu{X}}\mkern 4.0mu4\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}

to some process
P
.
Depending on the argument to which this function is applied,
P
should behave very differently:
if it is applied to
P
itself, it should receive something from
Q
.
However, if it’s applied to any other term, it should do nothing.
We therefore project the choreography above to the following program for
P
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}

Note that the
AmI
construct is necessary for process polymorphism in general, unless process variables cannot be instantiated to the process they are located at. It, and the combinatorial explosion caused by having multiple process abstractions, is not caused by the choreographic language but instead the choreographic language hides it and lets programmers avoid explicitly describing both sides of the
AmI
separately.

Note that we do not have a kinding system for local programs.
In fact, we do not check the types of local programs at all.
However, because types have
computational
content, we need to project them as well.
In order to preserve that computational content, we again use an equivalence of types which corresponds to
\beta,\eta
-equivalence.
However, in order to accommodate
AmI
types, we must index that equivalence with a process.
Then, we have two rules regarding
AmI
types:

\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{AmI}}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}?}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}}\quad\textsc{left=IAm}\frac{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\neq\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{AmI}}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}?}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\&}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}}\quad\textsc{left=IAmNot}

Now that we have seen the syntax of the programs which run on each process, we can look at whole networks:

Definition 1
.

A network
\mathcal{N}
is a finite map from a set of processes to local programs.
We often write
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}1}}}}[{L_{1}}]\mid\cdots\mid{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}n}}}}[{L_{n}}]
for the network where process
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}i}}}
has behaviour
L_{i}
.

The parallel composition of two networks
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
with disjoint domains,

\mathcal{N}\mid\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, simply assigns to each process its behaviour in the network defining it.
Any network is equivalent to a parallel composition of networks with singleton domain, as suggested by the syntax above.

We now consider the operational semantics of local programs and networks.
These are given via labelled-transition systems; the syntax of both sorts of label can be found in Figure
5
.
Selected rules for both operational semantics can be found in Figure
6
.
The full rules can be found in Appendix
C
. As before, transitions are indexed by a set
d
of function definitions.
Function variables reduce by looking up their definition in
d
.
Since this transition involves no communication, it is labelled with the empty transition,
\tau
.

Local Language:

\frac{}{f\xrightarrow{\tau}_{d}d(f)}\quad\textsc{left=NDef}\\
\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}\xrightarrow{\tau}_{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}}\quad\textsc{left=NBot}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}\xrightarrow{\tau}_{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}}\quad\textsc{left=NBott}\frac{}{\mathsf{sub}[\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}]\mkern 4.0muV\xrightarrow{\tau}_{d}{V}[\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}]}\quad\textsc{left=NSub}\\
\frac{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}L_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}L_{2}\xrightarrow{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}_{d}L_{1}}\quad\textsc{left=NAmIR}\frac{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\neq\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}L_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}L_{2}\xrightarrow{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}_{d}L_{2}}\quad\textsc{left=NAmIL}\\
\frac{}{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\oplus}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}~\ell~L}\xrightarrow{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\oplus}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}~\ell~}}_{d}L}\quad\textsc{left=NCho}\frac{}{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}~\{\ell_{1}:L_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:L_{n}\}\xrightarrow{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}~\ell_{i}}_{d}L_{i}}\quad\textsc{left=Noff}\frac{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mkern 4.0muV_{1}\xrightarrow{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mkern 4.0muV_{1}~V_{2}}_{d}V_{2}}\quad\textsc{left=NSend}\frac{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mkern 4.0muV_{1}\xrightarrow{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mkern 4.0muV_{2}~V_{1}}_{d}V_{2}}\quad\textsc{left=NRecv}

Networks:

\frac{L_{1}\xrightarrow{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mkern 4.0muV~({V^{\prime}}[\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}])}_{d}L^{\prime}_{1}\quad L_{2}\xrightarrow{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}\mkern 4.0mu({V}[\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}])~V^{\prime}}_{d}L^{\prime}_{2}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}}}}[L_{1}]\mid\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}}}}[L_{2}]\xrightarrow{\tau_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}},\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}_{d}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}}}}[L^{\prime}_{1}]\mid\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}}}}[L^{\prime}_{2}]}\quad\textsc{left=NCom}\\
\frac{L_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\oplus}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}~\ell~}}_{d}L^{\prime}_{1}\quad L_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}~\ell}_{d}L^{\prime}_{2}}{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}[L_{1}]\mid{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[L_{2}]\xrightarrow{\tau_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}},\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}_{d}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}[L^{\prime}_{1}]\mid{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[L^{\prime}_{2}]}\quad\textsc{left=NSel}\\
\frac{L\xrightarrow{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}_{d}L^{\prime}}{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[L]\xrightarrow{\tau_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}_{d}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[L^{\prime}]}\quad\textsc{left=NProam}\frac{L\xrightarrow{\tau}_{d}L^{\prime}}{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[{L}]\xrightarrow{\tau_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}_{d}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[{L^{\prime}}]}\quad\textsc{left=NPro}\frac{\mathcal{N}_{1}\xrightarrow{\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}}_{d}\mathcal{N}_{2}}{\mathcal{N}_{1}\mid\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}}_{d}\mathcal{N}_{2}\mid\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{left=NPar}\\

Figure 6:
Semantics of Networks (Selected Rules)

Perhaps surprisingly, undefined arguments to functions do not immediately cause the application to be undefined.
To see why, think about choreographies of the form
({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM)\mkern 4.0muN
where some process
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\neq\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
is involved in both
M
and
N
.
We project this to an application on
Q
of the form
({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}})\mkern 4.0mu\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}
.
Note that because we know that
N
has type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
, the projection
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}
has type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
and eventually evaluates to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
.
Thus, if
({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}})\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
immediately evaluated to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
, the process
Q
could not participate in
M
, as they need to do!
We therefore allow this to evaluate to
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}
.
However, when the function is also undefined, we evaluate this to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
with the empty label
\tau
, as you can see in the rules
[
NBot
]
and
[
NBott
]

As mentioned earlier, the explicit substitutions
\mathsf{sub}[\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}]
are functions which, when applied, perform the requested substitution in the value to which they are applied.
This is implemented in the rule
[
NSub
]
.

The
AmI
terms are given meaning via the rules
[
NAmIR
]
and
[
NAmIL
]
.
The rule
[
NAmIR
]
says that the term
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}L_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}L_{2}
can evaluate to
L_{1}
with label
P
, while the rule
[
NAmIL
]
says that it can instead reduce to
L_{2}
with label
Q
where
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\neq\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
.
We will see later that in the network semantics, we only allow transitions labeled with the process performing the transition.

Choice and offer terms reduce via the rules
[
NCho
]
and
[
Noff
]
.
The first,
[
Ncho
]
, tells us that a choice term simply reduces to its continuation with a transition label indicating the choice that has been made.
The second,
[
Noff
]
, tells us that an offer term can reduce to
any
continuation, with a transition label indicating the label of the continuation it reduced to.
We will see later that the semantics of networks only allows the offer term to reduce to the continuation chosen by a matching choice term.

Finally, the
send
and
recv
terms are given meaning via
[
NSend
]
and
[
NRecv
]
, respectively.
However, these rules behave somewhat-differently than might be expected: rather than acting as a plain send and receive, they behave more like a swap of information.

In a plain send, the sender would not have any information after the send—perhaps the term would come with a continuation, but this would not be related to the send.
Moreover, the receiver would not provide any information, but merely receive the information from the sender.
However, when sending a choreography with process polymorphism, the sender may need to participate in the continuation, depending on how polymorphic functions are applied.
For instance, consider the following choreography, where
P
sends a polymorphic function to
Q
, and the resulting polymorphic function is applied to
P
:

(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}Y\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}\mkern 4.0mu({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}Y^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}Y^{\prime}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{X}}\mkern 4.0mu(5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})))\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}

The polymorphic function that results from the
com
above is as follows:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\left(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}Y^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}Y^{\prime}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}},\mkern 2.0mu{X}}\mkern 4.0mu(5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}})\right)

Applying this to
P
leads to a program where
P
receives from
Q
.
Since
P
needs to participate in this program,
P
must have a program remaining after sending the polymorphic function to
Q
.

While this explains why
send
terms cannot simply, for instance, return unit, it does not explain why
send
and
recv
terms
swap
results.
To see this, consider what happens when a term is sent from a process
P
to another process
Q
.
We know from our type system that
Q
is not mentioned in the type of the term being sent, and we know that after the send all mentions of
P
are changed to mentions of
Q
.
Hence, after the send,
P
’s version of the term should be the view of a process not involved in the term.
This is exactly what
Q
’s version of the term is
before
the send.
Thus,
send
s and
recv
s behaving as swaps leads to the correct behaviour.

Now that we have discussed the semantics of local programs, we discuss the semantics of networks.
Each transition in the network semantics has a silent label indexed with the processes participating in that reduction:
\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}
consists of either one process name (for local actions at that process) or two process names (for interactions involving these two processes). We treat
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}
as a set, implicitly allowing for exchange.

For instance, the rule
[
NCom
]
describes communication.
Here, one local term must reduce with a
send
label, while another reduces with a
recv
label.
These labels must match, in the sense that the value received by the
recv
must be the value sent by the
send
—though with the receiver in place of the sender—and vice-versa.
Then, a network in which the local terms are associated with the appropriate processes,
Q
and
P
, can reduce with the label
\tau_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}},\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
.
Similarly, the rules
[
NSel
]
reduces matching choice and select terms, resulting in the label
\tau_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}},\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
.

While
[
NCom
]
and
[
NSel
]
describe communication, the rest of the rules describe how a single process’s term can evolve over time in a network.
Particularly interesting is
[
NProam
]
, which says that a
AmI
term can reduce only according to the process it is associated with.
We can see here that the resulting label is
\tau_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
, indicating that this reduction step only involves
P
.

The rules
[
NPro
]
tells us how to lift steps with an empty label
\tau
.
Such steps make no assumptions about the network, and so such terms can be associated with any process
P
.
When such a reduction takes place in a network, we label the resulting transition
\tau_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
.

Finally, the rule
[
NPar
]
says that if one part of a network can reduce with a label
\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}
, then the entire network can reduce with that same label.
This allows the other rules, which assume minimal networks, to be applied in larger networks. In the future we will use
\to^{*}
and
\to^{+}
to denote respectively a sequence and a sequence of at least one action with arbitrary labels.

4.1
Projection

We can now define the endpoint projection (EPP) of choreographies.
This describes a single process’s view of the choreography; the concurrent interpretation of a choreography is given by composing the projection to every process in parallel.
Endpoint projection to a particular process
P
is defined as a recursive function over typing derivations
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
.
For readability, however, we write it as a recursive function over the term
M
, and use the notation
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}N{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})}
to refer to the types assigned to any term
N
in the implicit typing derivation.
Similarly, we use
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{kindof}(}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44})}
to refer to the kind of a type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
in the implicit typing derivation.
We write
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
to denote the projection of the term
M
(implicitly a typing derivation for
M
, proving that it has
some
type) to the process
P
.

Intuitively, projection translates a choreography term to its corresponding local behavior.
For example, a communication action projects to a send (for the sender), a receive (for the receiver), a substitution (for the other processes in the type of the value being communicated) or an empty process (for the remaining processes).
However, this is more complicated for
case
statements.
For instance, consider the following choreography, which matches on a sum type which is either an integer on
Alice
or nothing on
Alice
.
If it is an integer, then
Bob
receives that integer from Alice and returns it.
Otherwise,
Bob
returns the default value of
42
.

Alice
informs
Bob
of which branch she has taken using
select
terms.

\left(\begin{array}[t]{l}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0muz\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}})\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}case}}\mkern 5.0muz\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}of}}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{~}\mkern 4.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Just}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}X}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}}}\mkern 4.0mux);\\
\mkern 10.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{~}\mkern 4.0muy\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{Nothing}\mkern 4.0mu(42\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}})\end{array}\end{array}\right)~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}}\mkern 4.0mu(3\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}})

Imagine projecting this to
Bob
’s point of view.
He does not have any of the information in the sum, so he cannot participate in choosing which branch of the
case
expression gets evaluated.
Instead, he has to wait for
Alice
to tell him which branch he is in.
If we naïvely translate just the first branch of the case expression,
Bob
waits for
Alice
to send him the label
Just
and then waits for
Alice
to send him an integer.
Similarly, in the second branch
Bob
waits for
Alice
to send him the label
Nothing
before returning the default value
42
.
Somehow, we need to combine these so that
Bob
waits for either label, and then takes the corresponding action.

We do this by
merging

Bob
’s local programs for each branch
(
Carbone et al. 2012
;
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2020
;
Honda et al. 2016
)
.
Merging is a
partial
function which combines two compatible local programs, combining choice statements.
In other words, the key property of merging is:

\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}~\{\ell_{i}:B_{i}\}_{i\in I}\sqcup\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}~\{\ell_{j}:B^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in J}=\\
\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}~\left(\{\ell_{k}:B_{k}\sqcup B^{\prime}_{k}\}_{k\in I\cap J}\cup\{\ell_{i}:B_{i}\}_{i\in I\setminus J}\cup\{\ell_{j}:B^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in J\setminus I}\right)

Merging is defined homomorphically on other terms, though it is undefined on incompatible terms.
Thus, for example,
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muM\sqcup\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0muN=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\mkern 4.0mu(M\sqcup N)
, but
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\mkern 4.0muM\sqcup\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\mkern 4.0muN
is undefined.

We can then use this to project the choreography above to
Bob
as:

({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0muz\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}}}~\{\textsf{Just}:(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot},\textsf{Nothing}:42\})~{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}

Where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
represents a part of the choreography executed by
Alice
.

Definition 2
.

The EPP of a choreography
M
for process
P
is defined by the rules in Figures
7
,
8
and
9
.

To project a network from a choreography, we therefore project the choreography for each process and combine the results in parallel:

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket=\prod_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}(M)}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\left[\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\right]
.

\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}&\text{if}~\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}x{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}\\

Figure 8:
Projection of PolyChor
\lambda
Programs (ctd.)

Intuitively, projecting a choreography to a process that is not involved in it returns a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
.
More complex choreographies, though, may involve processes that are not shown in their type.
This explains the first clause for projecting an application: even if
P
does not appear in the type of
M
, it may participate in interactions inside
M
.
A similar observation applies to the projection of
case
, where merging is also used.

Selections and communications follow the intuition given above, with one interesting detail:
self-selections are ignored, and self-communications project to the identity function.
This is different from many other choreography calculi, where self-communications are not allowed—we do not want to impose this in PolyChor
\lambda
, since we have process polymorphism and therefore do not want to place unnecessary restrictions on which processes a choreography can be instantiated with.

Any process
P
must prepare two behaviours for a process abstraction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM
:
one for when
X
is instantiated with
P
itself, and one for when
X
is instantiated with another process.
To do this, we use
AmI
terms, which allow
P
to use its knowledge of its identity to select which behaviour takes place.
(This also holds when the kind of
X
is restricted, as long as the base kind is
Proc
, though if
P
is excluded from the type of
X
and
P
does not participate in
M
then we simply project to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
.)
However, type abstractions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM
do not use
AmI
terms if
K
is not a kind of processes, since
P
cannot instantiate
X
.

In general, projecting a type yields
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
at any process not used in that type. We use the restrictions on kinds to avoid projecting type variables and type abstractions when we know we do not need to and project all process names to themselves, but otherwise the projection of type constructs is similar to that of corresponding process terms.

Finally, to execute a projected choreography, we need to project the set of definitions of choreographic functions to a set of definitions of local functions.
Since these functions are all parametrised over every involved process, this is as simple as projecting the definitions onto an arbitrarily chosen process name.

\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket=\{f\mapsto\left\llbracket{D(f)}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mid f\in\mathsf{domain}(D)\}\}

Note that function names always get projected everywhere.
This means that if we have a function which does not terminate when applied to some value in any process, then it diverges when applied to that value in the choreography and in every other process.

Example 2
.

We will now show how to project the bookseller service example from Section
1
. As in that example we use
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0mux=M~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~M^{\prime}
as syntactic sugar for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM^{\prime}~M
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
and
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0muM_{1}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0muM_{2}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0muM_{3}
as syntactic sugar for
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}case}}\mkern 5.0muM_{1}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}of}}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{~}\mkern 4.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}M_{2};~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{~}\mkern 4.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}M_{3}
for some
x\notin(\text{fv}(M_{2})\cup\text{fv}(M_{3}))
. We project for
Seller
and get the following process:

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muB\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~B\\
~\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{title}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0mux=({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0muy\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muy)\mkern 4.0mu\textsf{title}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muy=({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0muz\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muz)\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}if}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mkern 4.0muy\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\\
~\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{title}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{buyAtPrice?}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\\
\mkern 10.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0mux=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}recv}}_{B}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muy=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{B}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux)\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{B}}~\{{\textsf{Buy}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}},{\textsf{Quit}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}}\}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Here we can see that if the buyer
B
turns out to be
Seller
itself, then all the communications become identity functions, and the seller does not inform itself of its choice. Otherwise, we get a function which, after being instantiated with a buyer, receives two
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
s representing values existing at
B
.
It then waits for
B
to send a title, returns the price of this title, and waits for
B
to decide whether to buy or not.
It might seem strange to have a function parametric on two
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
s, but this example actually illustrates why we cannot necessarily remove the first two
\lambda
s from the process without causing a deadlock.
Consider that
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}let}}\mkern 5.0muy=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{B}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux)\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}in}}~\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{B}}~\{{\textsf{Buy}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}},{\textsf{Quit}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}}\}

is syntactic sugar for
({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0muy\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{B}}~\{{\textsf{Buy}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}},{\textsf{Quit}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}}\})~(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{B}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux))
.
Here we need to have the abstraction on
y
even though it gets instantiated as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
after
Seller
sends the result of
\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux
to
B
.
If instead we only had
({\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{B}}~\{{\textsf{Buy}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}},{\textsf{Quit}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}()}}\}})~(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}send}}_{B}\mkern 4.0mu(\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux)),

the first part of the application would not be a value, and would be waiting for
B
to choose between
Buy
and
Quit
while
B
has the abstraction on
y
and therefore considers the first part of the application a function which must wait to be instantiated.

B
therefore expects to receive the result of
\textsf{price\_lookup}\mkern 4.0mux
, and we get a deadlock in our system.
This is why we never want to project a value to a non-value term, and need to keep any abstractions guarding a part of the choreography involving
Seller
.

\left\llbracket{X}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{kindof}(}X{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44})}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho})\mathrel{\text{for}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\\
X&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}()}&\text{if}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}&\text{if}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}&\text{if}~\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}\\
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}+}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}&\text{if}~\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}\\

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\begin{array}[]{l}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\\[10.00002pt]
