Figure 9:
Projection of PolyChor
\lambda
Types

5
The Correctness of Endpoint Projection

We now show that there is a close correspondence between the executions of choreographies and of
their projections.
Intuitively, this correspondence states that a choreography can execute an action if, and only if, its projection can execute the same action, and both transition to new terms in the same relation.
However, this is not completely true: if a choreography
M
reduces by rule
[
CaseL
]
, then the result has fewer branches than the network obtained by performing the corresponding reduction in the projection of
C
.

In order to capture this we revert to the branching relation
(
Montesi 2023
;
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2020
)
, defined by
M\sqsupseteq N
iff
M\sqcup N=M
.
Intuitively, if
M\sqsupseteq N
, then
M
offers the same and possibly more behaviours than
N
.
This notion extends to networks by defining
\mathcal{N}\sqsupseteq\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
to mean that, for any role
P
,
\mathcal{N}(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})\sqsupseteq\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})
.

Using this, we can show that the EPP of a choreography can do all that (completeness) and only what (soundness) the original choreography does. For traditional imperative choreographic languages, this correspondence takes the form of one action in the choreography corresponding to one action in the projected network. We instead have a correspondence between one action in the choreography and multiple actions in the network due to allowing choreographies to manipulate distributed values in one action such as in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\mathsf{Int}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muM\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}3\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Bob}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0mu3\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}@}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Alice}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
where both
Bob
and
Alice
independently take the first part of the pair.

Theorem 5

(Completeness)
.

Given a closed choreography
M
, if
M\rightarrow_{D}M^{\prime}
,
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash D
,
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
, and
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket
is defined, then
there exist networks
\mathcal{N}
and
M^{\prime\prime}
such that:

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rightarrow^{+}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\mathcal{N}
and

\mathcal{N}\sqsupseteq\left\llbracket{M^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket
.

In the foregoing we use
L
to denote local expressions and
U
to denote local values in order to make the proofs more readable, as we will be switching back and forth between layers a lot.

Before we can prove completeness, we need a few lemmas. First, we show that choreographic values always project to local values.

Lemma 3
.

For any choreographic value
V
and process
P
, if
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash V:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
then
\llbracket V\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
is either a value or undefined.

Proof.

Straightforward from the projection rules.
∎

We then prove the same for type values.

Lemma 4
.

Given a type value
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
, if
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
then for any process
P
in
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}})
,
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
.

Proof.

Straightforward from the projection rules.
∎

We then show that type values are projected to
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
at uninvolved processes.

Lemma 5
.

Given a type value
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\neq\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
, for any process
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu})
,
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
.

Proof.

Straightforward from induction on
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
.
∎

And similarly, we show that choreographic values project to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
at processes not involved in their type.

Lemma 6
.

Given a value
V
, if
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash V:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
then for any process
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau})
, we have
\llbracket V\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
or
\llbracket V\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
is undefined.

Proof.

Follows from Lemmas
3
and
5
and the projection rules.
∎

Finally, we show that equivalent types are projected to equivalent local types.

Lemma 7
.

Given a closed type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
, if
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}^{\prime}
and
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
, then
for any process
P
,
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
.

Proof.

We prove this by structural induction on
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}^{\prime}
. All but one case follow by simple induction.

The one interesting case is if
T={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}[X:={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}]
. Then (1) if
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}(\{R\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho})
and
\left\llbracket{T[1]}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
, we have
\left\llbracket{T}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\llbracket{T^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
. (2) If
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}},{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\}
then
\left\llbracket{T}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}[X:={\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}})~\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
and
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}[X:={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}]}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
. Since
T
is a closed type and
\Theta\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
must be a process. If
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
then obviously
({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}[X:={\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}})~\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}[X:={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}]}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\neq\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}
then
({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\forall}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}[X:={\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}})~\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[X:=\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]
, but since
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
is a process
Q
,
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}
and
\left\llbracket{X}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=X
, and therefore we get
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
.
And (3) otherwise we have
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\right\rrbracket_{=}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}~\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
and
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[X:=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]
. We therefore get
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
.
∎

We are now ready to prove completeness.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We prove this by structural induction on
M\rightarrow_{D}M^{\prime}
.

•

Assume
M={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muN~V
and
M^{\prime}=N[x:=V]
. Then for any process
P
such that
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
or
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
, we have
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}})~\llbracket V\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
and
\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[x:=\llbracket V\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]
, and for any other
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}
, we have
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}V{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})})
and therefore
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}=\left\llbracket{M^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
. We therefore get
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]\xrightarrow{\tau}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
for all
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muN{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})}
and define
\mathcal{N}=\prod\limits_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muN{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})})}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]\mid\prod\limits_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muN){\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}]
and the result follows.

•

Assume
M={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muN~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
, and
M^{\prime}=N[X:={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}]
.
Then if
K\in\{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}},\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\}
, for any process
P
,
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}::}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}\left\llbracket{N[t:={\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}~\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}
and the result follows Lemmas
4
and
7
, and Rules
[
NBabs
]
,
[
NIamr
]
, and
[
NIaml
]
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\notin\{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}},{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\}
then for any process
P
such that
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}(\{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho})
, we have
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}=\left\llbracket{M^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
, for any other
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}

we have
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Lambda}\mkern 3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0mu\llbracket N\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}})~\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}
and
\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}[X:=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}]
. We therefore get
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]\rightarrow^{*}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
for all
P
and the result follows.

•

Assume
M=N~M^{\prime\prime}
,
M^{\prime}=N^{\prime}~M^{\prime\prime}
, and
N\rightarrow_{D}N^{\prime}
.
Then for any process
P
such that
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}=\left\llbracket{M^{\prime\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
, by induction we have
\left\llbracket{N^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
, and therefore
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}=\left\llbracket{M^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
.
For any process
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}
such that
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}N{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})})
or
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}\neq\left\llbracket{M^{\prime\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}
,
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}~\llbracket M^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}
and
\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}=\llbracket N^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}~\llbracket M^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}
.
For any other process
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
, by induction we get
\left\llbracket{N^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
and therefore
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}=\left\llbracket{M^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}=\left\llbracket{M^{\prime\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}
.
For any other process
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that
\left\llbracket{M^{\prime\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
, we get
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
and
\left\llbracket{M^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=\left\llbracket{N^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
.
And by induction
\llbracket N\rrbracket\rightarrow^{*}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\mathcal{N}_{N}

\mathcal{N}_{N}\sqsupseteq\left\llbracket{N^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket
.
For any process
P
we therefore get
\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\xrightarrow{\mu_{0}}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\xrightarrow{\mu_{1}}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\dots L_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
for
L_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\sqsupseteq\left\llbracket{N^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}
for some sequences of transitions
\xrightarrow{\mu_{0}}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\xrightarrow{\mu_{1}}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\dots
, and from the network semantics we get

\llbracket M\rrbracket\rightarrow^{*}\begin{array}[]{l}\prod\limits_{\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}=\left\llbracket{M^{\prime\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}]\mid\prod\limits_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}N{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})})\text{ or }\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}\neq\left\llbracket{M^{\prime\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}[L_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}~\llbracket M^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}]\\
\mid\prod\limits_{\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}=\left\llbracket{M^{\prime\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}[\left\llbracket{M^{\prime\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}]\mid\prod\limits_{\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}[L_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}]\\
\end{array}=\mathcal{N}

and

M^{\prime}\rightarrow_{D}N^{\prime}~M^{\prime\prime}
.
And since
\llbracket N\rrbracket\rightarrow^{*}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
and
\llbracket N^{\prime}\rrbracket\rightarrow^{*}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{N}
, we know these sequences of transitions can synchronise when necessary, and if
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}\neq\left\llbracket{N^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
then we can do the extra application to get rid of this unit.

M=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}select}}_{{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}},{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}}\mkern 4.0mu\ell\mkern 4.0muM^{\prime}
. Then
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}=\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\oplus}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}~\ell~\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}}
,
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}=\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}~\{\ell:\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}\}
, and for any
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}\notin\{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\}
,
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}=\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}
. We therefore get
\llbracket M\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\tau_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\llbracket M\rrbracket\setminus\{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}\}\mid{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}]\mid{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}[\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}]
and the result follows.

•

Assume
M={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}N{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muN^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
,
N\rightarrow_{D}N^{\prime\prime}
, and
M^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}N^{\prime\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muN^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
. Then the result follows from simple induction.

•

Assume
M={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}V{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muN{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
,
N\rightarrow_{D}N^{\prime}
, and
M^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}V{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muN^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
. Then the result follows from simple induction.

•

Assume
M=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}fst}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}V{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muV^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
and
M^{\prime}=V
. Then for any process
P
such that
\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
or
\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
,
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}fst}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0mu\llbracket V^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
and for any other process
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}M^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muV^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})}
, we have
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
and
\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
. We define
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket
and the result follows.

•

Assume
M=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}snd}}\mkern 4.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}V{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muV^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
and
M^{\prime}=V^{\prime}
. Then the case is similar to the previous.

•

Assume
M=f
and
M^{\prime}=D(f)
. Then the result follows from the definition of
\llbracket D\rrbracket
.∎

Theorem 6

(Soundness)
.

Given a closed choreography
M
and a function mapping
D
, if
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}
,
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash D
, and

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rightarrow^{*}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\mathcal{N}
for some network
\mathcal{N}
, then
there exist a choreography
M^{\prime}
and a network
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
such that:
M\rightarrow^{*}_{D}M^{\prime}
,

\mathcal{N}\rightarrow^{*}\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}\sqsupseteq\left\llbracket{M^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket
.

As with completeness, we need some ancillary lemmas before we can prove soundness. For this, we need a notion of removing processes from a network.

Definition 3
.

Given a network
\mathcal{N}=\prod\limits_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[L_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]
, we have
\mathcal{N}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}^{\prime}=\prod\limits_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\in({\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}^{\prime})}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[L_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]

First we show that actions in a network do not affect the roles not mentioned in the transition label.

Lemma 8
.

For any process
P
and network
\mathcal{N}
, if
\mathcal{N}\xrightarrow{\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}}_{d}\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
and
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}
then
\mathcal{N}(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}})
.

Proof.

Straightforward from the network semantics.
∎

Then we show that removing processes from a network does not prevent it from performing actions involving different processes.

Lemma 9
.

For any set of processes
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
and network
\mathcal{N}
, if
\mathcal{N}\xrightarrow{\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}}\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}=\emptyset
then
\mathcal{N}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\xrightarrow{\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}}_{d}\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}
.

Proof.

Straightforward from the network semantics.
∎

We finally show that if the projection of a choreographic type is equivalent to a local type value, then the original choreographic type is equivalent to a choreographic type value.

Lemma 10
.

Given a closed type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
and process
P
, if
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
and

\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
, then
there exist a type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}^{\prime}
such that:
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}^{\prime}
and
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}
.

Proof.

We prove this by structural induction on
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
. All but one case follows from simple induction.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}
. Then if
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
, we have
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
and therefore
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}^{\prime}
.
Otherwise, if
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{kindof}(}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44})}={\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}(\{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho})
, we get
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
and the result follows form induction.
Otherwise if
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}
, we get
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
and the result follows from induction.
Otherwise, we get
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}~\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
. By induction,
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
and there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}^{\prime}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}^{\prime}
and
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}
and
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}
and there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}^{\prime}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}^{\prime}
and
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}
. Because
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}1}}
is kindable, we have a kind
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime}
such that
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}::{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\Rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}
and
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}::{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime}}
This means that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime}{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}4}}}
and if
{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{K}}^{\prime}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}},{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\textsf{Proc}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.82,0,0.44}\setminus}}{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\rho}\}
then
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}AmI}}~X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}?}}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}4}}}[X:=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}]}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\&}}\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}4}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
, otherwise
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}2}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\lambda}X\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}.}\mkern 3.0mu\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}4}}}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
. We then get
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}\equiv_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}4}}^{\prime}}[X:={{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}}]}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}^{\prime}\equiv{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}4}}^{\prime}}[X:={{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}^{\prime}}]
, and since
X
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}^{\prime}
are both base types, so are
\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}4}}^{\prime}}[X:={{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}}]}\right\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}4}}^{\prime}}[X:={{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\nu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}3}}^{\prime}}]
.
∎

We are then ready to prove soundness.

Proof of Theorem
6
.

We prove this by structural induction on
M
.

•

Assume
M=V
. Then for any process
P
,
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=U
, and therefore
\llbracket M\rrbracket\not\xrightarrow{\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}}
.

•

Assume
M=N_{1}~N_{2}
. Then for any process
P
such that
\left\llbracket{N_{1}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}=\left\llbracket{N_{2}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
, we have
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
.
For any process
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}
such that
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}N_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})})
or
\left\llbracket{N_{1}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}\neq\left\llbracket{N_{2}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}
,
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}=\llbracket N_{1}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}~\llbracket N_{2}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}
.
For any other process
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\left\llbracket{N_{2}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
, we get
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}=\left\llbracket{N_{1}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime}}
.
For any other process
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
, we get
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=\left\llbracket{N_{2}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
. We then have 2 cases.

–

Assume
N_{2}=V
. Then
\llbracket N_{2}\rrbracket_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}=U
by Theorem
3
, and for any
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}
such that
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}N_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\textsf{typeof}(}N_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82})})
, by Theorem
6
,
\llbracket N_{2}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
and therefore
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}=\left\llbracket{N_{1}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime}}
, and we have 5 cases.

*

Assume
N_{1}={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\lambda}\mkern 3.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}:}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}.}\mkern 3.0muN_{3}
. Then for any process
P
such that
\left\llbracket{N_{3}}\right\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\bot}
or

. This case is similar to the previous.

•

Assume
M={\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}(}M_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11},}\mkern 2.0muM_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11})}
. Then the result follows from simple induction.

•

Assume
M=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}case}}\mkern 5.0muN\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}of}}~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inl}}_{~}\mkern 4.0mux\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}N^{\prime};~\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}inr}}_{~}\mkern 4.0mux^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}\Rightarrow}}N^{\prime\prime}

, we have

. For any other process
{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
, we have
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}=(\lambda x:{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\bot}.\llbracket N^{\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}\sqcup\llbracket N^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}})~\llbracket N\rrbracket_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}
. We have two cases.

–

Assume

. Then we have three cases.

From Theorems

,
4
,
5
, and
6
, we get the following corollary, which states that a network derived from a well-typed closed choreography can continue to reduce until all roles contain only local values.

Corollary 1
.

Given a closed choreography
M
and a function environment
D
containing all the function names of

M
, if
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash M:T
and
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash D
, then: whenever

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rightarrow^{*}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\mathcal{N}
for some network
\mathcal{N}
, either there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}
such that

\mathcal{N}\xrightarrow{\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\mathcal{P}}}}_{\left\llbracket{D}\right\rrbracket}\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
or

\mathcal{N}=\prod\limits_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}\textsf{ip}}(M)}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}[V_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}}]
.

6
Related Work

6.1
Choreographies

Choreographies are inspired by the “Alice and Bob” notation for security protocols by
Needham and Schroeder 1978
, which included a term for expressing a communication from a participant to another.
The same idea inspired later several graphical notations for modelling interactions among processes, including sequence diagrams and message sequence charts
(
Object Management Group 2017
;
International Telecommunication Union 1996
)
.

The first sophisticated languages for expressing choreographies were invented to describe interactions between web services.
The Web Services Choreography Description Language (WS-CDL) by the W3C
(
(2004) W3C
)
is a choreographic language which describes the expected observable interactions between web services from a global point of view
(
Zongyan et al. 2007
)
.

Carbone et al. 2012
later formalized endpoint projection for a theory of choreographies based on WS-CDL, and in particular introduced the merging operator (which we adjusted and extended to our setting).
This inspired more work on choreographies and projection and eventually the birth of choreographic programming—where choreographies are programs compiled to executable code—and the first choreographic programming language, Chor
(
Montesi 2013
)
.
As choreographic programming languages became more complex,
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2020
developed a
core calculus of choreographies
(CC).
Montesi 2023
revisited and generalised CC in his text on foundations of choreographic languages.
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021
then formalized this new version and its properties in the Coq theorem prover
(
The Coq development team 2004
)
. Later,
Pohjola et al. 2022
developed a certified end-to-end compiler from another variation of CC to CakeML by using the HOL theorem prover.

One of the primary design goals of all of choreographic programming languages is
deadlock-freedom by design

(
Carbone and Montesi 2013
)
—the operational correspondence between the choreography and the distributed network ensures deadlock-freedom for the network.
PolyChor
\lambda
achieves this goal.

The first choreographic language with limited process polymorphism was Procedural Choreographies (PC)
(
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2017
)
.
In PC, a choreography comes with an environment of predefined
procedures
parametric on process names which may be called by the choreography.
These procedures have a number of limitations compared to the process polymorphism of PolyChor
\lambda
: they cannot contain any free processes, they cannot be partially instantiated, and they are lower-order—that is, they must be defined in the environment rather than as part of a larger choreography.
These limitations allow the projection function to know how the procedure will be instantiated, whereas in PolyChor
\lambda
we may need to compute the processes involved first. This has major implications for projection: in PC, it is easy to tell when projecting a procedure call which processes are involved and therefore need a local copy of the call.
However, PolyChor
\lambda
’s full process polymorphism allows the function and process names to each be enclosed in a context.
While this allows greater flexibility for programmers, it forces us to project a process-polymorphic functions to every process and let each process determine at runtime whether it is involved.

Recently, there has been a fair amount of interest in higher-order programming for choreographies
(
Giallorenzo et al. 2020
;
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021
)
.
The first higher-order choreographic programming language, Choral
(
Giallorenzo et al. 2020
)
, is an object-oriented choreographic language compiled to Java code.
Thus, Choral choreographies can depend on other choreographies, allowing programmers to reuse code. Choral was also the first choreographic language to treat
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}com}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}\tau}}_{{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}P}}},\mkern 2.0mu{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.74,0.39,0}Q}}}}
as a first-class function.

While Choral gave a practical implementation of higher-order choreographies, it did not establish their theoretical properties.
Two different—but independently-developed—works filled this gap, including Chor
\lambda
, the basis of PolyChor
\lambda
.
Chor
\lambda
is a functional choreographic calculus based on the
\lambda
-calculus.
In this work, we extended Chor
\lambda
with type and process polymorphism and the ability to send non-local values such as choreographies.
Chor
\lambda
, and hence PolyChor
\lambda
, provides a core language for higher-order choreographies, thus allowing us to establish their properties.
Since the original Chor
\lambda
has parametric procedures like PC and Choral, it lacks PolyChor
\lambda
’s property that a choreography diverging in one process must diverge in every process.
This forces Chor
\lambda
to have both a complex notion of out-of-order execution and a more lax correspondence between actions in the network and the choreography.

The other work establishing the theoretical properties of higher-order choreographic programming is Pirouette
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
)
, which is also a functional choreographic programming language based on simply-typed
\lambda
calculus.
Unlike Chor
\lambda
(and thus PolyChor
\lambda
), Pirouette does not allow processes to send messages written in Pirouette.
Instead, it takes inspiration from lower-order choreographic programming languages in which (the computations to produce) messages are written in their own separate language.
Like the choreographic languages in
(
Montesi 2023
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021
)
, Pirouette’s design is parametrized by the language for writing messages. Thus, Pirouette can describe communication patterns between processes that draw from a large swath of languages for their local computations.
Nevertheless, this design means that Pirouette fundamentally cannot allow programs to send choreographic functions, unlike PolyChor
\lambda
.

Moreover, unlike Chor
\lambda
and PolyChor
\lambda
, Pirouette forces every process to synchronize when applying a function.
This conceit allows Pirouette to establish a bisimulation relation with its network programming language, a result formalized in Coq.
This result allows a traditional—and verified—proof of deadlock-freedom by construction.
However, this constant synchronization would be a bottleneck in real-world systems; PolyChor
\lambda
’s choice to obtain a weaker—but strong-enough—connection between the languages allows it to avoid this high cost.

6.2
Concurrent Functional Programming

Functional concurrent programming has a long history, starting with attempts to parallelize executions of functional programs
(
Burton 1987
)
.
The first language for functional programming with communications on channels was Facile
(
Giacalone et al. 1989
)
which, unlike later choreographic languages, had an abstraction over
process IDs
very similar to process polymorphism.
A more recent work, which more-closely resembles choreographic programming, is Links
(
Cooper et al. 2006
)
, with the RPC calculus
(
Cooper and Wadler 2009
)
as its core language.
Links and the RPC calculus, like choreographies, allow a programmer to describe programs where different parts of the computation takes place at different locations and then compile it to separate code for each location.
Interestingly, though Links has explicit communication, in the RPC calculus the top level does not, and communications are created only in the projection when projecting a function located at a different process.
Moreover, the RPC calculus does not feature multiple threads of computation; instead, on communication the single thread of computation moves to a new location while other locations block.
The RPC calculus was later extended with location polymorphism, very similar to our and Facile’s process polymorphism
(
Choi et al. 2020
)
. However, as the RPC calculus only deals with systems of 2 processes, a client and a server, they project a process abstraction as a pair, and then the location as picking the correct part of this pair. This solution creates a simpler network language but is not suitable for a framework with an arbitrary number of participants such as PolyChor
\lambda
.
Moreover, the RPC calculus—like PolyChor
\lambda
but unlike traditional choreographic languages—does not have out-of-order execution at the top level.

Session types were applied to a concurrent functional calculus with asynchronous communication by
Gay and Vasconcelos 2010
.
However, their session types do not give them a deadlock-freedom result.
Instead, they get a
runtime safety
result: the only time a thread cannot perform an action is if it has terminated, is trying to read from an empty buffer, or is trying to start a new channel with another thread which isn’t ready yet.

ML5
(
Licata and Harper 2010
;
Murphy VII et al. 2007
)
is a functional concurrent programming language based on the semantics of modal logic.
However, instead of the
send
and
recv
terms of choreographic languages, they have a primitive get[
w
]
M
, which makes another process
w
evaluate
M
and return the result.
Since
M
may include other gets, this construct gives ML5 something resembling PolyChor
\lambda
’s ability to send a full choreography.
However, the result of evaluating this “choreography” must be at the receiver and then returned to the sender.

Multitier
programming languages, like ScalaLoci
(
Weisenburger and Salvaneschi 2020
)
, offer another paradigm for describing global views of distributed systems.
Like Choral, ScalaLoci is built on top of an existing object-oriented language: in this case, Scala.
In ScalaLoci and other
tierless
languages, an object describes a whole system containing multiple processes and functions.
Differently from choreographic programming, multitier programming does not allow for modelling the intended flow of communications. Rather, communication happens implicitly through remote function calls and the concrete protocol to be followed is largely left to be decided by a runtime middleware.
For a more detailed comparison of choreographic and multitier languages, see the work of
Giallorenzo et al. 2021
.

7
Conclusion

In this paper, we presented PolyChor
\lambda
, the first higher-order choreographic programming language with process polymorphism.
PolyChor
\lambda
has a type and kind system based on System F
\omega
, but extended such that process names are types of kind
Proc
.
Moreover, we showed how to obtain a concurrent interpretation of PolyChor
\lambda
programs in a process language by using a new construct corresponding by the ability of a process to know its identity. We found that this construct was necessary if process variables are able to be instantiated as the process they are located at, but using a choreographic language abstracts from this necessity.
Our explorations of process polymorphism also allowed PolyChor
\lambda
to describe a communication of a non-local value from
P
to
Q
as sending the part of the message owned by
P
to
Q
. These non-local values include full choreographies, creating a simple and flexible way to describe delegation by communicating a distributed function describing the delegated task. This innovation required a new notion of communication as an exchange in which the delegator rather than being left with an empty value after sending a choreography is left with a function which will allow it to potentially take part in the delegated task, e.g. by receiving a result at the end.

Process polymorphism fills much of the gap between previous works on the theory of higher-order choreographies and practical languages.
However, there is still more work to do.
For instance, currently PolyChor
\lambda
does not support recursive types.
In order to support recursive types, we would need to either make endpoint projection capable of projecting to a possibly-nonterminating description of a process, or limit recursive types ability to make type computations fail to terminate.

Furthermore, one can imagine allowing processes to send types and process names as well as values.
This would, for example, allow us to program a server to wait to receive the name of a client which it will have to interact with.
Since this form of delegation is common in practice, understanding how to provide this capability in a choreographic language, while retaining the guarantees of choreographic programming, would enable programmers to apply their usual programming patterns to choreographic code.

We project local type despite lacking a typing system for local processes. Our unusual network communication semantics have made it difficult to define local typing rules for
send
s and
recv
s, and we therefore leave local typing (or alternatively type erasure) as future work.

Finally, while certain, instant, and synchronous communication is convenient for theoretical study, such assumptions do not match real-world distributed systems.

Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2017
modeled asynchronous communication in choreographies via runtime terms representing messages in transit.
We could adapt this method to PolyChor
\lambda
by having the communication primitive reduce in two steps: first to a runtime term and then to the delivered value.
However, this extension would be nontrivial, since it is not clear how to represent messages in transit when those messages are non-local values such as choreographies.

While these gaps between theory and practice persist, process polymorphism in PolyChor
\lambda
brings us much closer to realistic choreographic languages for distributed systems.
Choreographic programs promise to provide easier and cleaner concurrent and distributed programming with better guarantees.
With higher-order choreographic programming and process polymorphism, the fulfilment of that promise is nearly within reach.

Appendix A
Full PolyChor
\lambda
Typing Rules
